To evaluate a prediction function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta :{{\mathbb {R}}}^K\rightarrow \{0,1\}$$\end{document}η:RK→{0,1} on D, we employ the balanced accuracy (BACC), which is defined to be\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {BACC}(\eta ,D)\triangleq \frac{1}{2}\big (\frac{\text {TP}(\eta ,D)}{\text {TP}(\eta ,D)+\text {FN}(\eta ,D)} + \frac{\text {TN}(\eta ,D)}{\text {TN}(\eta ,D)+\text {FP}(\eta ,D)} \big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}BACC(η,D)≜12(TP(η,D)TP(η,D)+FN(η,D)+TN(η,D)TN(η,D)+FP(η,D)).We construct prediction functions based on Lasso linear regression (LLR) [48], decision tree (DT) [49], artificial neural networks (ANN) [50] and random forest (RF) [51]. We evaluate the performance of each learning model by means of 10 repetitions of 5-fold cross validation. Specifically, for each property \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi$$\end{document}π, we divide the data set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_\pi$$\end{document}Dπ into 5 subsets randomly, say \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_{\pi ,1},\dots ,D_{\pi ,5}$$\end{document}Dπ,1,⋯,Dπ,5, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|D_{\pi ,i}|-|D_{\pi ,j}|\le 1$$\end{document}|Dπ,i|-|Dπ,j|≤1 holds for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i,j=1,2,\dots ,5$$\end{document}i,j=1,2,⋯,5. For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1,2,\dots ,5$$\end{document}i=1,2,⋯,5, we construct a prediction function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document}η from a subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_\pi \setminus D_{\pi ,i}$$\end{document}Dπ\Dπ,i as the training set, based on different data sets, evaluate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {R}^2(\eta ,D_{\pi ,i})$$\end{document}R2(η,Dπ,i) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {BACC}(\eta ,D_{\pi ,i})$$\end{document}BACC(η,Dπ,i) on the remaining subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_{\pi ,i}$$\end{document}Dπ,i as the test set. We take as the evaluation criterion the median of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$5\times 10=50$$\end{document}5×10=50 values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {R}^2$$\end{document}R2 and BACC observed over 10 repetitions of 5-fold cross validation. It should be emphasized that for binary classification tasks, the prediction function constructed by DT, RF and ANN can directly classify the property class as negative or positive, but when the prediction function is constructed by LLR, the predicted value obtained by the test data is a real number, which cannot be used directly to classify the property class. Let us denote the training set by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_t$$\end{document}Dt and denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_1$$\end{document}C1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0 as the number of positive and negative molecules in the training set, respectively. We use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}θ to denote the classification threshold, which is defined to be\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta \triangleq \underset{r \in R}{\arg \max }\ \, \widetilde{\text {BACC}}(\eta ,r), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θ≜argmaxr∈RBACC~(η,r),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R\triangleq \{\eta ({{\mathbb {C}}}) \mid {{\mathbb {C}}}\in D_t\}$$\end{document}R≜{η(C)∣C∈Dt} and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \widetilde{\text {BACC}}(\eta ,r)\triangleq \frac{1}{2}\big (\frac{\widetilde{\text {TP}}(\eta ,r)}{C_1} + \frac{\widetilde{\text {TN}}(\eta ,r)}{C_0} \big ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}BACC~(η,r)≜12(TP~(η,r)C1+TN~(η,r)C0),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\text {TP}}(\eta ,r) \triangleq |\{{{\mathbb {C}}}\in D_t \mid a({{\mathbb {C}}})=1,\eta ({{\mathbb {C}}}) \ge r \}|$$\end{document}TP~(η,r)≜|{C∈Dt∣a(C)=1,η(C)≥r}| and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\text {TN}}(\eta ,r) \triangleq |\{{{\mathbb {C}}}\in D_t \mid a({{\mathbb {C}}})=0,\eta ({{\mathbb {C}}}) \le r \}|$$\end{document}TN~(η,r)≜|{C∈Dt∣a(C)=0,η(C)≤r}|. When it holds that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \le \eta ({{\mathbb {C}}})$$\end{document}θ≤η(C), we regard the property class of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathbb {C}}}$$\end{document}C as positive.

We show the results in Table 5 and Table 6. We may say that we can construct a good prediction function for many data sets; for the regression data sets, in 19 (resp., 11) out of the 27 data sets, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {R}^2$$\end{document}R2 over 0.8 (resp., 0.9) is achieved. We observe that, for some data sets, there is a learning model that is not suitable. For example, LLR and ANN perform poorly for Vp, regardless of feature functions. Notably, ANN performs extremely poorly, yielding an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {R}^2$$\end{document}R2 value of -100, indicating its ineffectiveness for this data set. In contrast, DT and RF are relatively good. For the classification data sets, we can see that the performance is rather good, in 13 (resp., 5) out of the 17 data sets since BACC score over 0.7 (resp., 0.8) is achieved.

Let us compare two feature functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\text {2L}}$$\end{document}f2L and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\text {2L+CC}}$$\end{document}f2L+CC for regression task and classification task respectively. For each property \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi$$\end{document}π, an underlined value indicates the maximum over the 8 values (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=$$\end{document}= 2 feature functions by 4 learning models). For regression task, the maximum is achieved for only 5 properties when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=f_{\text {2L}}$$\end{document}f=f2L, whereas it is up to 26 properties when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=f_{\text {2L+CC}}$$\end{document}f=f2L+CC. For classification task, the maximum is achieved for 1 properties when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=f_{\text {2L}}$$\end{document}f=f2L, whereas it is up to 16 properties when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=f_{\text {2L+CC}}$$\end{document}f=f2L+CC. In both Table 5 and Table 6, bold-face (resp., *) indicates an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {R}^2$$\end{document}R2 value or a BACC value that is larger at least by 0.02 (resp., 0.05) than the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {R}^2$$\end{document}R2 value or a BACC value achieved by the other feature function and the same learning model. For example, for At, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {R}^2$$\end{document}R2 value 0.401 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\text {2L+CC}}$$\end{document}f2L+CC and RF is bold since it is larger than 0.379 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\text {2L}}$$\end{document}f2L and RF by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.401-0.379=0.022>0.02$$\end{document}0.401-0.379=0.022>0.02. In Table 5, a bold value (resp., *) appears only twice (resp., nowhere) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=f_{\text {2L}}$$\end{document}f=f2L, whereas it appears 41 (resp., 21) times when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=f_{\text {2L+CC}}$$\end{document}f=f2L+CC. In Table 6, a bold value (resp., *) appears nowhere (resp., nowhere) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=f_{\text {2L}}$$\end{document}f=f2L, whereas it appears 24 (resp., 10) times when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=f_{\text {2L+CC}}$$\end{document}f=f2L+CC.

We conclude that, in the 2L model, the learning performance of a prediction function can be improved by introducing CC descriptors.

Inference experiments (stage 4)

For a given property, after deciding two reals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\underline{y}}^*,{\overline{y}}^*\in {{\mathbb {R}}}$$\end{document}y_∗,y¯∗∈R and a specification \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document}σ, we solve the MILP for inferring a chemical graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathbb {C}}}^\dagger$$\end{document}C†. Recall that the MILP consists of two families of constraints, that is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({{\mathcal {C}}}_1)$$\end{document}(C1) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\underline{y}}^*\le \eta (x)\le {\overline{y}}^*$$\end{document}y_∗≤η(x)≤y¯∗ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x=f({{\mathbb {C}}}^\dagger )$$\end{document}x=f(C†); and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({{\mathcal {C}}}_2)$$\end{document}(C2) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathbb {C}}}\in {{\mathcal {G}}}_\sigma$$\end{document}C∈Gσ. In this experiment, for the regression datasets, we employ a hyperplane that is learned by LLR for the prediction function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document}η. A hyperplane is a prediction function that is represented by a pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(w,b)\in {{\mathbb {R}}}^K\times {{\mathbb {R}}}$$\end{document}(w,b)∈RK×R and predicts the property value of a feature vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in {{\mathbb {R}}}^K$$\end{document}x∈RK by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w(1)x(1)+\dots +w(K)x(K)+b$$\end{document}w(1)x(1)+⋯+w(K)x(K)+b. Hence, the constraint \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\underline{y}}^*\le \eta (x)\le {\overline{y}}^*$$\end{document}y_∗≤η(x)≤y¯∗ in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal {C}}}_1$$\end{document}C1 is represented by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\underline{y}}^*\le \sum _{j=1}^Kw(j)x(j)+b\le {\overline{y}}^*, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}y_∗≤∑j=1Kw(j)x(j)+b≤y¯∗,where use of a hyperplane in mol-infer was proposed in [29]. In classification tasks, we also use the hyperplane constructed by LLR as the prediction function. When we aim to infer molecules that exhibit positive for the target property, the constraint in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal {C}}}_1$$\end{document}C1 is represented by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta \le \sum _{j=1}^Kw(j)x(j)+b. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θ≤∑j=1Kw(j)x(j)+b.When we aim to infer molecules that exhibit negative for the target property, the constraint in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal {C}}}_1$$\end{document}C1 is represented by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{j=1}^Kw(j)x(j)+b \le \theta , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑j=1Kw(j)x(j)+b≤θ,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}θ is the classification threshold. For the other constraints, see Appendix B.

In regression data sets, we take up two properties Kow and OptR, for which LLR achieves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {R}^2$$\end{document}R2 over 0.9. In classification data sets, we take up two properties AhR and AR_LBD, for which LLR achieves BACC score over 0.8. We consider 10 specifications that have seed trees non-isomorphic to each other, where 9 out of the 10 seed trees are shown in Fig. 4. These seed trees are introduced to observe how computation time changes with respect to the number of nodes; the number of ring edges; and the tree structure. The last seed tree is the one in Fig. 3(a). We denote this seed tree by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal {T}}}_{5^*}$$\end{document}T5∗. In each of the 10 specifications, we set other parameters (see Table 2) than the seed tree sufficiently large to the extent of the data set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_\pi$$\end{document}Dπ. For example, we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal {F}}}^u:={{\mathcal {F}}}(D_\pi )$$\end{document}Fu:=F(Dπ) for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in T$$\end{document}u∈T, that is, all fringe trees that appear in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_\pi$$\end{document}Dπ are available to u.Fig. 4Seed trees for the inference experiments: All nodes are ring nodes. A ring edge (resp., a non-ring edge) is depicted by a thick (resp., thin) line

Seed trees for the inference experiments: All nodes are ring nodes. A ring edge (resp., a non-ring edge) is depicted by a thick (resp., thin) line

Statistics of MILPs for AR_LBD: Targets are set to Positive

We highlight the practical efficiency of our MILP formulation with CC descriptors, as shown in Tables 7 to 10. Across a range of seed trees and properties, chemical graphs with up to 50 non-hydrogen atoms were inferred within a practical time—most of the formulations were solved in less than one minute, a few were solved in about five minutes and only one was solved in around 18 min. There is a tendency such that computation time is longer when there are more #V/#C (i.e., the numbers of variables/constraints in MILP) with some exceptions. For example, for Kow, the case of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal {T}}}_{\text {4b,2}}$$\end{document}T4b,2 takes 38.4 s, which is much more than the cases where there are six ring nodes. Concerning #V/#C, the more the ring nodes, the more they become. The #V/#C are equal between seed trees if they have the same numbers of ring nodes/edges; e.g., #V/#C are equal between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal {T}}}_{\text {4a}}$$\end{document}T4a and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal {T}}}_{\text {4b,0}}$$\end{document}T4b,0. These results indicate that adding CC descriptors does not lead to too much computational overhead in practice.

We also show some of the inferred chemical graphs in Fig. 5. As expected, ring nodes in the seed trees are expanded to cycles in the chemical graphs. We acknowledge that some of the inferred graphs seem unstable and hard to synthesize (e.g., 4-cycles or ionized elements). This is a common issue in molecular inference studies [53]. We can prevent MILP from using such structures by setting specifications appropriately. Addressing this issue is left as future work.Fig. 5Inferred chemical graphs

Inferred chemical graphs

Conclusions

In this paper, we proposed a new family of descriptors, cycle-configurations, that can be used in the standard 2L model of mol-infer. We introduced the definition in Sect. '“Cycle-Configurations'' and described how we deal with them in the MILP in Sect. “MILP Formulation for 2L+CC Model''. Then in Sect. “Results and Discussion'', we demonstrated that the performance of a prediction function is improved in many cases when we introduce CC descriptors. We also showed that a chemical graph with up to 50 non-hydrogen atoms can be inferred in a practical time.

The 2L+CC model can be extended further in the similar way as the 2L model. Specifically, we can enumerate isomers of the inferred graph by dynamic programming [54] or generate “close” compounds in the sense of property values by a grid neighborhood approach [55]. Note that the constraint \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal {C}}}_1$$\end{document}C1 of the MILP can contain multiple prediction functions for multiple properties, as is done in [29], where we have included a single property in this paper for simplicity. Besides, we may apply the 2L+CC model to inference of polymers [36]. These are left for future work.
